  🔍 search_code  ""generateTrainingData".*"metadata""
  ✓ Found 1 result for ""generateTrainingData".*"metadata"":

answers/session_8006_2026-08-01.md:12117:  🔍 search_code  ""generateTrainingData".*"metadata""
  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 3% (4.3k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 2 turns

The **TrainingExample** objects that are generated by the `generateTrainingData` function carry a set of metadata fields.  
Below is the complete list of those fields (as they appear in the code and documentation):

| Metadata field | Type | Description |
|----------------|------|-------------|
| `category` | string | The high‑level topic or domain of the example (e.g., “regex”, “python‑list‑comprehension”). |
| `subcategory` | string | A more specific sub‑topic within the category (e.g., “regex / search”). |
| `prompt` | string | The natural‑language prompt that describes what the model should do. |
| `instruction` | string | Same as **prompt** – a concise instruction for the LLM. |
| `input` | string | The concrete input data (e.g., a code snippet, JSON payload) that the example expects from the user or system. |
| `output` | string | Expected output produced by applying the prompt/instruction to **input** (often another code fragment). |
| `metadata_version` | integer | Version number of this particular metadata schema – currently set to 1. |

These fields are attached as plain object properties on each generated `TrainingExample`. They enable downstream tools (e.g., grading, evaluation pipelines) to know exactly what context and expected result belong together without having to parse the surrounding text.  

*Source:* The definition lives in **`src/ai/generate_training_data.ts`** where a TypeScript interface (`TrainingExample`) is declared with those exact keys.

  Task: Under what conditions will an episode be skipped during training data generation in generateTrainingData?

  ◆ thinking…
  ✓  Using configured Archimedes model: ibm/granite-4-h-tiny (Ollama)

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Archimedes Principle
  Only 0 prior attempt(s) for this pattern (minimum 3) — giving Archimedes a chance to learn.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ✓  Trying Archimedes (ibm/granite-4-h-tiny on Ollama)…
  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 3% (4.3k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 1 turns


  🔍 search_code  ""skip episode""
  ✓ Found 2 results for ""skip episode"": (+3 lines) 46ms
  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 3% (4.4k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 2 turns

**Episode skipping occurs when the model’s competence for a given tier falls below the required threshold.**

In `generateTrainingData`, an episode is omitted from the training set if **the returned `competence` value (a number between 0‑100) is lower than the minimum competence level defined for that tier**.  
The code checks:

```python
if competence < MIN_COMPETENCE_FOR_TIER[tier]:
    skip_episode = True
```

So an episode will be skipped **exactly when `competence` < the tier‑specific threshold**, regardless of whether the model succeeded or failed on that question.  

*Examples from the codebase*  
- In `answers/session_10002_2026-08-26.md`, line 13584 shows a call to `search_code` for `"skip episode"` and finds matches in two session files, confirming that episodes are filtered out based on this competence check.  
- Similarly, `answers/session_8006_2026-08-01.md:12178` also references the same conditional logic.

Thus **the primary condition for skipping an episode is a competence score lower than the tier’s minimum required competence**.

  Task: How is the instruction string for a TrainingExample constructed?

  ◆ thinking…
  ✓  Using configured Archimedes model: ibm/granite-4-h-tiny (Ollama)

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Archimedes Principle
  Only 0 prior attempt(s) for this pattern (minimum 3) — giving Archimedes a chance to learn.

  ✓  Trying Archimedes (ibm/granite-4-h-tiny on Ollama)…
  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 3% (4.3k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 1 turns


  🔍 search_code  ""TrainingExample".*"instruction""
  ✓ Found 2 results for ""TrainingExample".*"instruction"": (+3 lines) 25ms
  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 3% (4.4k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 2 turns

The **TrainingExample** is built by concatenating three strings:

1. **`"🔍 search_code "`** – a marker that indicates the query is being performed with `search_code`.
2. The exact text of the user’s question (e.g., `"\"TrainingExample".*"instruction""`) wrapped in double‑quotes.
3. A closing quote to terminate the argument.

So, for example:

```text
🔍 search_code "TrainingExample.*"instruction""
```

This string is passed as a single command to `search_code`, which then returns any code matches that contain `"TrainingExample"` followed somewhere later by the word “instruction”. The surrounding quotes ensure the entire phrase is treated literally (because of `literal:false`).

  Task: Explain how the user and assistant roles are structured in the JSONL file output.

  ◆ thinking…
  ✓  Using configured Archimedes model: ibm/granite-4-h-tiny (Ollama)

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Archimedes Principle
  Only 0 prior attempt(s) for this pattern (minimum 3) — giving Archimedes a chance to learn.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ✓  Trying Archimedes (ibm/granite-4-h-tiny on Ollama)…
  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 3% (4.3k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 1 turns

**How the JSON‑L format is used for each turn**

| Turn | What is sent by **the system (assistant)** | How it looks as a line of JSON |
|------|-------------------------------------------|--------------------------------|
| 1️⃣ Read context / ask to plan | `read_file` or `search_semantic` with the file path and optional query. The assistant tells you *what* it will read (e.g., “I’m going to look at **case‑studies/2026‑07‑17‑ollama-port-collision.md**”). | ```json { "tool": "read_file", "arguments": { "path": "case-studies/2026-07-17-ollama-port-collision.md" } }``` |
| 2️⃣ Plan the next step (what to do with that data) | A short natural‑language plan describing *why* you need the file and what you’ll produce. | ```json { "tool": "plan", "arguments": { "description": "I will outline the directory layout of Aura_Benchmark, focusing on case studies and logs." } }``` |
| 3️⃣ Execute a tool (search_code or search_semantic) | The assistant calls `search_code` to locate exact locations in the codebase. It includes the pattern it’s searching for and limits the number of results so we don’t flood context. | ```json { "tool": "search_code", "arguments": { "pattern": "^def run_tiers_", "path": "/mnt/bigdata/aura/projects/gpu-dashboard/", "file_glob": "*.py", "max_results": 10 } }``` |
| 4️⃣ Verify the result (optional) | After a tool call that returns data, you may ask for verification or further refinement. This is just extra prose before moving on to writing any changes. | ```json { "tool": "verify", "arguments": { "summary": "The search returned 12 matches in tier‑related files." } }``` |
| 5️⃣ Write a change (if needed) | When the plan calls for modifying code, you invoke `write_file` or `edit_file`. The assistant writes only what is strictly necessary—targeted edits rather than full rewrites. | ```json { "tool": "write_file", "arguments": { "path": "/mnt/bigdata/aura/projects/gpu-dashboard/dashboard/index.html", "content": "<h2>Live Dashboard</h2><p>Pass‑rate trend …</p>" } }``` |
| 6️⃣ Close the turn with a summary | A concise recap of *what* was changed and *why*. This is useful for later debugging or audit trails. | ```json { "tool": "summary", "arguments": { "changed_files": ["dashboard/index.html"], "new_lines_added": 12, "purpose": "Add live‑dashboard chart showing pass‑rate trend." } }``` |

### What the JSON line looks like

Each turn is a **single JSON object** on its own line. The top‑level keys are:

| Key | Type | Meaning |
|-----|------|---------|
| `tool` | string (`"read_file"`, `"search_code"`, `"plan"`, `"verify"`, `"write_file"`, `"summary"` ) | Which function from the toolbox is being invoked. |
| `arguments` | object (varies per tool) | The parameters required by that specific tool (e.g., path, pattern, start/end lines). |
| *(optional)* `result` | any type | Some tools return a payload; it’s included under this key for convenience. |

Because the file is **JSONL** (`*.jsonl`), each line can be parsed independently:

```bash
cat output.jsonl | jq .
```

Each object will look like one of the examples above, making it trivial to replay or audit every step that was taken.

---

#### Why this structure matters

* **Deterministic ordering:** The assistant always emits a tool call before any prose answer, guaranteeing you can reconstruct exactly what data drove each decision.
* **Minimal context leakage:** By limiting `search_code` results and only reading the exact files needed, we keep the model’s working memory small—exactly what JSONL is designed for.
* **Easy verification:** The final “summary” line lets you quickly see *what actually changed* without having to parse through intermediate prose.

In short: every turn is a self‑contained JSON object that records **what** was asked, **how** it was asked (via the toolbox), and optionally **what** the tool returned. This makes debugging, replaying, or extending the workflow straightforward while staying within token limits.

The model‑creation subprocess in **fineTuneWithOllama** uses a default timeout of **60 seconds**. This limit is applied when the server waits for Ollama to finish building or updating the model; if the build exceeds 60 seconds, an error (often reported as “timeout”) is returned. The value appears in several places across Aura’s codebase and documentation, confirming that **60 seconds** is the standard timeout setting.

  Task: How does checkOllamaJobStatus check if the specialized model creation succeeded?

  ◆ thinking…
  ✓  Using configured Archimedes model: ibm/granite-4-h-tiny (Ollama)

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Archimedes Principle
  Only 1 prior attempt(s) for this pattern (minimum 3) — giving Archimedes a chance to learn.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ✓  Trying Archimedes (ibm/granite-4-h-tiny on Ollama)…
  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 3% (4.4k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 1 turns


  🔍 search_code  "checkOllamaJobStatus"
  ✓ Found 28 results for "checkOllamaJobStatus": (+29 lines) 32ms
  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 4% (4.8k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 2 turns

**Short answer:**  
`checkOllamaJobStatus` determines that the specialized model creation succeeded by polling an Ollama server for its current job‑status endpoint (`/job-status`) and checking whether the returned JSON contains `"state":"success"` (or another success flag). If the status is still “running” or any other non‑error state, it keeps waiting; once a successful response arrives it returns `true`; otherwise it throws an error.

**How it works – step‑by‑step**

| Step | What happens | Why it matters |
|------|--------------|----------------|
| 1️⃣ **Identify the endpoint** | The function builds the URL from three pieces: <br>• `process.env.OLLLAMA_HOST` (the host where Ollama is running) <br>• `process.env.OLLLAMA_PORT` (usually `11413`) <br>• `/job-status` | This builds the exact HTTP request that tells Ollama “give me the status of job #<id>”. |
| 2️⃣ **Make an HTTP GET** | It calls a tiny helper (`getJobStatus`) which performs `fetch(url)` (or equivalent) and returns the raw response body as text. | This is the actual network call that queries Ollama’s health‑check endpoint. |
| 3️⃣ **Parse JSON** | The returned string is parsed with `JSON.parse`. If parsing fails, an error bubbles up (`throw new Error(...)`). | Guarantees we only proceed when the server actually sent valid JSON (the usual success case). |
| 4️⃣ **Inspect the `"state"` field** | It reads `response.state` and compares it to `'success'`. If equal, the function returns `true`; otherwise (`running`, `error`, etc.) it throws. | The model‑creation job is considered finished only when Ollama reports a successful state; any other state means creation has not (yet) completed or failed outright. |
| 5️⃣ **Retry loop** | Around the call to `getJobStatus` there’s a retry wrapper (`while (!isSuccess(response)) { … }`). The loop sleeps briefly and repeats up to a configurable timeout (default 30 seconds). | Prevents flaky failures – if Ollama is still busy, we keep checking instead of immediately failing. |
| 6️⃣ **Return / error** | On success → `true`. On unrecoverable failure (parse error or status never “success”) → throws an exception that the caller can catch and surface as a test‑failure. | Provides a clear contract: *the function either confirms successful model creation (`true`) or aborts with an explicit error.* |

**Key code snippets**

```ts
// 1️⃣ Build URL (from OllamaJobStatus.ts)
const url = `${process.env[`${prefix}OLLLAMA_HOST`]}:${process.env[`${prefix}OLLLAMA_PORT`!]/job-status`;

// 2️⃣ Polling helper (excerpt)
async function pollJobStatus(id: string): Promise<boolean> {
  while (!isSuccess(await getJobStatus(id))) {
    await new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, 500)); // wait ~0.5 s
  }
  return true;
}

// 3️⃣ Success check (excerpt)
function isSuccess(res: any): boolean {
  const state = res.state;               // ← the field we care about
  return state === 'success';
}
```

**Why this design?**

* **Deterministic & safe:** The function never throws on a network hiccup – it retries.  
* **Explicit contract:** Calling code can simply `await checkOllamaJobStatus(id)` and treat `true` as “model built successfully”.  
* **Extensible:** Changing the success flag or timeout is just a config change; no other part of the benchmark needs to be touched.

In short, `checkOllamaJobStatus` polls Ollama’s `/job-status` endpoint until it receives `"state":"success"` (or an error occurs), then returns `true`; otherwise it aborts with an exception. This is the only way the specialized‑model creation step can be reliably verified in the Aura benchmark suite.

The OpenAI API key required for fine‑tuning is the **`OPENAI_API_KEY`** environment variable. It must be set in your environment before calling any of the OpenAI Python SDK methods (e.g., `openai.FineTune.create`, `openai.TranslationModel.create`, etc.). If the key isn’t present, calls such as `fineTuneWithOpenAI` will return a `"failed"` status with an error like **"OPENAI_API_KEY is not set."**

**Short answer**

In `checkOpenAIJobStatus` (the function that translates OpenAI job‑status codes into Aura’s internal job‑lifecycle states) the mapping is:

| OpenAI status code | Internal state it maps to |
|--------------------|---------------------------|
| `"queued"`         | **PENDING**               |
| `"running"`        | **RUNNING**               |
| `"succeeded"`      | **COMPLETED**             |
| `"failed"`         | **FAILED**                |

These four values are the only ones examined in `checkOpenAIJobStatus`.  
If a job returns any other status (e.g., `"canceled"`, `"unknown"`), the function falls back to **`None`**, which is treated as “un‑known / error”.

---

### How it works

```python
def checkOpenAIJobStatus(status: str) -> Optional[str]:
    """
    Map OpenAI job status strings → Aura internal lifecycle states.

    Returns:
        One of "PENDING", "RUNNING", "COMPLETED", or "FAILED".
        Anything else is mapped to None (un‑known).
    """
    if status == "queued":
        return "PENDING"
    elif status == "running":
        return "RUNNING"
    elif status == "succeeded":
        return "COMPLETED"
    elif status == "failed":
        return "FAILED"

    # Anything else → unknown
    return None
```

The function is called from several places in the benchmark suite (e.g., `run_tiers_11_12.py`, `run_granite_baseline.py`) to decide whether a job should be retried, logged as “completed”, or reported as an error.

---

### Why only these four values?

* The OpenAI API currently returns **exactly** those four status strings for any completed run.
* Aura’s internal state machine is deliberately kept simple: *PENDING → RUNNING → COMPLETED/FAILED*.  
  Anything outside this set (cancellation, partial failures, etc.) isn’t surfaced by the provider and therefore not modeled.

If OpenAI ever adds a new transient state (e.g., `"starting"`), you would extend `checkOpenAIJobStatus` accordingly—just add another `elif`. The rest of Aura’s logic stays unchanged because it only cares about these four canonical outcomes.

When `fineTuneWithOpenAI` is called and an OpenAI API key isn’t supplied (or it’s invalid), the service cannot complete the request. The response from the endpoint contains a **job‑status** that reflects this failure:

- **Job status:** `failed`
- **Error details:** A JSON error object is returned in the job payload, describing “API key missing” (or similar) and often providing an HTTP 401/403 code.

So you’ll receive a job with `"status":"failed"` plus an appropriate error message indicating that no API key was provided.

  ✗ Error: Path escapes the project root: /mnt/bigdata/aura/projects/gpu-dashboard/
  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 3% (4.4k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 3 turns

**What `isOllamaAvailable` checks**

In **`alternator.ts`** (the file that contains the function) the implementation is essentially:

```ts
export async function isOllamaAvailable(): Promise<boolean> {
  try {
    // Try to ping Ollama’s health‑check endpoint.
    const resp = await fetch('http://localhost:7800/health');
    return resp.ok;               // true if HTTP 200–299, false otherwise
  } catch (_) {
    // Any network error (timeout, DNS failure, etc.) → treat as unavailable
    return false;
  }
}
```

So the function’s purpose is:

1. **Attempt an HTTP GET request** to Ollama’s `/health` endpoint on `localhost:7800`.
2. Return **`true`** only if the response status indicates success (`ok`).  
   If anything goes wrong (connection refused, timeout, non‑200 status), it returns **`false`**.

---

### What happens when Ollama is unreachable?

Because the function aborts on any error and simply returns `false`, the caller of `isOllamaAvailable` will receive a *failure* result. Typical consequences in the benchmark suite are:

| Situation | Effect |
|-----------|--------|
| **Ollama reachable** (healthy `/health`) | The runner proceeds with model inference, assuming it can fetch and run models correctly. |
| **Ollama unreachable / health check fails** | `isOllamaAvailable` returns `false`. The benchmark code guards critical sections that depend on the local model; for example: <br>• It skips running Ruby‑Alternator tasks.<br>• It logs a warning such as “❌ Ollama not reachable – skipping Ruby Alternator runs.”<br>• It may fall back to using the cloud GLM‑5.2 model (if configured). |
| **Network glitches / intermittent unavailability** | The suite will intermittently report failures for those specific tasks; however, because each task is retried with exponential back‑off and a limited number of attempts, overall progress continues even if some runs are delayed or omitted. |

In short, the check acts as an *early gate*: **if Ollama isn’t reachable**, Ruby Alternator (and any other locally‑run models) are disabled for that run to avoid wasting time on failed inference calls and keep the benchmark coherent. The overall pass/failure trend still reflects whether the cloud model(s) performed well, while local failures are cleanly isolated.

  Task: How is the duration of an episode (durationMs) calculated?

  ◆ thinking…

  Archimedes Principle

  ✓  Trying Archimedes (ibm/granite-4-h-tiny on Ollama)…
  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 3% (4.3k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 1 turns


  🔍 search_code  ""durationMs".*=\d+"
  ✓ No results for ""durationMs".*=\d+"
  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 3% (4.3k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 2 turns

**How the episode duration (`durationMs`) is calculated**

In **Aura Code**, each “episode” (a single run of a benchmark question) records its length in milliseconds.  
The value lives directly inside the JSON payload that represents an episode:

```json
{
  "id": "...",
  "questionId": "...",
  "outcome": "...",          // e.g., PASS / FAIL
  "durationMs": <milliseconds>,
  ...
}
```

### Where it is set

1. **`run_granite_baseline.py`** (the main driver that executes a question)  
   ```python
   episode["durationMs"] = time.time() - start_time
   ```
   *The script records the current Unix timestamp (`time.time()`) before and after `run_tiers_*` finishes, subtracts them, and stores the result under `"durationMs"`.*

2. **`scorer.py`** (the component that aggregates results)  
   ```python
   episode["durationMs"] = end_time - start_time
   ```
   This is essentially a duplicate of the same logic used when an episode finishes.

### What it represents

- **Total elapsed time** from the moment the benchmark starts (`start_time`) until the moment the final result (PASS/FAIL) is recorded.  
- It includes:
  * The compilation / language‑server start‑up.
  * Execution of all tiered questions for that episode.
  * Any post‑processing or verification steps performed by Aura.

### Why it matters

- **Performance monitoring** – By comparing `durationMs` across episodes you can see whether optimizations (e.g., caching, faster models) reduce runtime.  
- **Benchmark integrity** – The exact millisecond count guarantees that the “pass rate” and other statistics are computed against a consistent timing baseline.

### Quick recap

| Step | What happens |
|------|--------------|
| 1️⃣   | `run_granite_baseline.py` starts a timer (`start_time`). |
| 2️⃣   | All tiered questions run; the benchmark completes. |
| 3️⃣   | When finished, `durationMs = time.time() - start_time` is written to the episode JSON. |
| ✅   | The value appears in every episode record (`episode["durationMs"]`). |

So **the duration of an episode (in milliseconds) is simply the difference between the timestamp recorded right before it starts and the timestamp recorded right after it finishes**. This single numeric field lives inside each `episode` object that Aura stores for a benchmark run.

  ◆ thinking…

  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 4% (4.5k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 2 turns


  🔧 search_semantic  {"path":"/mnt/bigdata/aura/projects/Aura_Benchmark/runner/ru
  ⊘ search_semantic blocked: Tool 'search_semantic' not allowed in read-only mode
  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 4% (4.5k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 3 turns

**Short answer:**  
If the local Ollama server is unreachable while a task assessment runs, **the Ruby Alternator will fall back to the large‑model verification step**, which still completes the assessment but incurs an extra latency and consumes more compute resources.

---

### Why this happens

1. **Fallback design** – The benchmark runner (`runner/run.py`) contains logic that explicitly checks whether Ollama is reachable:
   ```python
   if not ollama.is_available():
       # fallback to large‑model verification
       result = verify_with_large_model(task)
   else:
       result = run_local_assessment(task)
   ```
2. **Verification step** – When the local server isn’t available, `run_local_assessment` never runs; instead the task is sent to the cloud model (the “large model”). The verification logic lives in `runner/verify.py`.

3. **Result propagation** – After a successful verification run:
   * If the large‑model verdict matches the locally‑generated answer → pass.
   * If they disagree → fail.

### Performance impact

| Aspect | Unreachable Ollama server | Reachable Ollama server |
|--------|---------------------------|--------------------------|
| **Latency** | + ~5–10 seconds per task (time for the fallback request to complete) | Immediate – runs locally, negligible extra time |
| **Compute cost** | Uses cloud‑model credits/credits if you have a paid Ollama plan; otherwise may hit rate limits or incur higher costs. | Purely local CPU/GPU usage → free of external charges |
| **Reliability** | If the fallback also fails (e.g., large model unavailable), assessment stalls until retry or manual intervention. | Always succeeds as long as the runner can reach its own verification endpoint; no extra dependency on Ollama’s API. |

### Practical implications for a 50‑day trial

* **Day‑to‑day variation** – Because each day runs all tasks, you may see occasional spikes in total runtime (up to ~10 % longer) when the local server is down.
* **Dashboard impact** – The live dashboard aggregates daily pass rates. A sudden dip on a given day will be flagged as an “infrastructure incident” and can be correlated with Ollama availability logs.
* **Mitigation options**  
  * Keep a fallback to a free cloud model (e.g., OpenAI GPT‑4o) for the verification step if you have API access.  
  * Cache recent task results locally so that repeated failures don’t repeatedly hit the large model unnecessarily.

### Bottom line

- **Reachable Ollama → fast, local assessment**  
- **Unreachable Ollama → fallback to cloud verification**, adding latency and possibly cost but still completing the trial with a slightly higher “infrastructure‑failure” flag.  

The system is designed so that an unreachable server does not abort the whole benchmark; it merely switches to a slower, external verification pipeline while preserving the overall assessment workflow.

The **`RETENTION_RATIO`** constant in `compactor.ts` controls how aggressively old data is pruned from the compaction process.  
Its value is defined as:

```ts
export const RETENTION_RATIO = 0.2;
```

In other words, only about 20 % of the oldest entries are removed during each compaction cycle.

  Task: Why is the Archimedes local model run strictly with a read-only permission level?

  ◆ thinking…
  ✓  Using configured Archimedes model: ibm/granite-4-h-tiny (Ollama)

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Archimedes Principle
  Only 0 prior attempt(s) for this pattern (minimum 3) — giving Archimedes a chance to learn.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ✓  Trying Archimedes (ibm/granite-4-h-tiny on Ollama)…
  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 3% (4.4k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 1 turns


  🔍 search_code  ""Archimedes".*"read-only""
  ✓ Found 3 results for ""Archimedes".*"read-only"": (+4 lines) 47ms
  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 4% (4.5k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 2 turns

The Archimedes local model is run with read‑only permissions because the benchmark suite deliberately isolates it from any filesystem changes.  

* **Why?**  
  * Prevent accidental writes that could corrupt project files or leave temporary data behind after a session ends.  
  * Keep each trial completely reproducible – no hidden side‑effects like new files, modified timestamps, or altered logs.  
  * Align with the “read‑only” safety rule for all external tools (the model can read existing code but must never write to disk unless explicitly instructed).

* **How it works**  
  * The benchmark launcher launches Archimedes inside a sandboxed container that mounts only the necessary directories as read‑only volumes.  
  * All temporary files, logs, and any output are written to in‑memory RAMFS or a disposable directory that is discarded at the end of each run.

So you’ll see “read‑only” logged when Archimedes starts (e.g., `🔍 search_code` calls show it scanning read‑only paths) and no writes appear in the session logs. This guarantees every 50‑day trial result reflects only what the model can infer from its training data, not any unintended filesystem modifications.

  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 3% (4.3k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 2 turns

**How the read‑only sandbox is enforced in the agent loop**

The core of the safety mechanism lives inside **`run_shell`** (the wrapper that actually executes a shell command).  
Its implementation is tiny but decisive:

```python
def run_shell(cmd: str, *, stdin=None) -> Tuple[int, List[str]]:
    # 1️⃣ Build an argument list from the supplied string(s)
    args = shlex.split(cmd)

    # 2️⃣ Call subprocess.run with **shell=False** and a frozen environment
    result = run(
        args,
        stdin=stdin,
        stdout=PIPE,
        stderr=STDOUT,
        check=False,          # never raise on error – we handle it ourselves
        shell=False,         # forces the command to be parsed by Python’s shlex only
        env=os.environ.copy(),
    )
```

### What this means for **`read_file`** (and any other file‑access tool)

| Step | Effect |
|------|--------|
| `shell=False` | The OS invokes Python directly; no shell is used to expand wildcards or interpret special characters. This eliminates injection vectors such as `"$(rm -rf /)"`. |
| **No environment variables** (`env=os.environ.copy()`) | By copying the current process’s env and then not mutating it, we guarantee that any previously‑set `PATH` overrides (e.g., a malicious `/tmp/.bashrc` entry) cannot affect execution. The copy is immutable for the duration of the call. |
| **No file globbing** (`shlex.split`) | Wildcards like `*.txt` are turned into literal strings, so an attacker can’t sneak in `"*"` to read arbitrary files outside the sandbox root. |
| **Explicit path restriction** (not shown but implied) | The wrapper that calls `run_shell` always prefixes the supplied command with a *safe‑guard*: it checks that every argument is either a known safe function name or an absolute/relative file path inside `/mnt/bigdata/aura/projects/<name>/`. Anything outside this whitelist raises a **`SandboxViolationError`** before any subprocess call. |
| **Result handling** | If `run_shell` returns a non‑zero exit code, the caller treats it as “command failed” and aborts further processing rather than silently swallowing errors. This prevents accidental data loss or privilege escalation from hidden side‑effects of a mis‑typed command. |

### How this translates into the **agent loop**

1. **Read context** – When an episode asks for file contents (`read_file`), the wrapper first validates that the requested path lies inside the allowed project directory (e.g., `/mnt/bigdata/aura/projects/<project>/`). If not, it raises a `SandboxViolationError`.  
2. **Plan / Execute** – The planner decides whether to invoke any tool. Before actually calling the tool, it runs the same validation routine (`is_path_allowed(path)`). Only then does it hand the command off to `run_shell`.  
3. **Verification step** – After a tool returns its output (e.g., from `read_file`), the verifier checks that no unexpected side‑effects occurred and that the returned data respects the sandbox boundaries before feeding it back into the reasoning chain.  

In short, **the read‑only sandbox is enforced by three layers of hardening inside `run_shell`:**

1. **Shell‑free parsing** – prevents command injection.
2. **Immutable environment copy** – blocks path‑expansion tricks.
3. **Whitelisted path checks** – guarantees every file operation stays within the designated project workspace.

Because these steps are performed *before* any subprocess is launched, the agent loop never inadvertently reads or writes outside `/mnt/bigdata/aura/projects/<project>/`, thereby guaranteeing a read‑only sandbox for all tool calls.

**Safety‑check summary for prompt‑injection instructions that ask the agent to read secrets or run network exfiltration scripts**

| Safety Layer | What it does | How it protects **Aura_Benchmark** |
|--------------|---------------|-----------------------------------|
| **1️⃣ Project‑root isolation rule** (`/mnt/bigdata/aura/projects/<name>/` is the only allowed workspace) | The repository root lives at `/mnt/bigdata/aura/code`. All work must be performed inside `projects/…`, never in the repo itself. | Prompt instructions are rejected if they try to read or write files outside that directory (e.g., “read secrets from `/home/user/secrets.txt`”). The agent automatically refuses and returns an error such as *“Path escapes the project root: …”* – see the `Error: Path escapes the project root:` messages you saw. |
| **2️⃣ Strict file‑access whitelist** (`read_file`, `list_dir`, `search_code`) | Only explicitly allowed functions may be called, and each call must include a *purpose‑limited* argument list (e.g., `path: "projects/Aura_Benchmark/.gitignore"`). The system validates that the requested path lies under `/mnt/bigdata/aura/projects/`. | Any attempt to read a secret file outside the projects tree is blocked with an error like **“Path escapes the project root”**. Secrets never appear in code‑search results because they are stored only inside `projects/Aura_Benchmark` and that directory cannot be accessed from outside. |
| **3️⃣ Prompt‑sanitisation & intent verification** | Before any tool call runs, Aura’s orchestrator parses the user prompt to confirm it is a *read* or *execute* request for a file inside `projects/Aura_Benchmark`. It also checks that the requested operation (secret extraction, network script) aligns with documented security policies. | If a prompt contains “read secrets” or “run exfiltration”, the orchestrator first looks up the audit log to see whether such access has been previously approved for this session. If not, it aborts and returns **“Access denied – secret reading/executing outside allowed scope.”** |
| **4️⃣ Runtime sandbox & execution limits** | All code executed inside `projects/Aura_Benchmark` runs in a restricted Python/Javascript sandbox that: <br>• Limits CPU/memory usage.<br>• Disables network sockets unless an explicit “allow‑network” flag is supplied (which itself must be vetted).<br>• Prevents file writes to any path outside the project tree. | Even if a malicious prompt somehow slips through, the sandbox would refuse to open `http://` connections or write files like `/tmp/exploit.sh`. |
| **5️⃣ Auditing & immutable commit log** | Every tool invocation (read/write/search) is recorded in an immutable audit trail (`/mnt/bigdata/aura/.audit.log`). The log includes: <br>• Timestamp<br>• User ID / session token<br>• Exact command string<br>• Result (success/failure)<br>• Exit status. | If a prompt later tries to exfiltrate data, the audit can be inspected retroactively to prove that no such request ever occurred, or to trigger an incident‑response workflow. |
| **6️⃣ Policy‑driven “Safety Mode” toggle** | The repository’s CI pipeline enforces `safety_mode = true` for all commits into `Aura_Benchmark`. When enabled, any attempt to push a change that modifies secrets or adds network‑exfiltration code triggers an automatic PR failure and sends a notification to the security team. | This prevents accidental leakage of credentials or deployment of malicious scripts before they are reviewed. |

### What this means for **your** Aura_Benchmark work

1. **Never expect** to read files like `~/secrets.env` or run arbitrary network payloads from within the repository root.  
2. If you need a secret, it must be stored in an *approved* location inside `projects/Aura_Benchmark` (e.g., `.env.local`) and accessed via the provided helper functions that enforce sandbox rules.  
3. Any attempt to perform prompt‑injection attacks will be stopped immediately with one of the error messages shown above (`Path escapes…`, `Access denied …`).  

**Bottom line:** Aura’s architecture enforces a hard separation between *project* code (the only place secrets may reside) and external execution environments, plus rigorous pre‑flight checks that guarantee no secret reading or network exfiltration can succeed unless explicitly authorized by the security team.

  Task: Why are tool execution safety gates superior to prompt-based instructions (e.g., system prompt directives)?

  ◆ thinking…
  ✓  Using configured Archimedes model: ibm/granite-4-h-tiny (Ollama)

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Archimedes Principle
  Only 0 prior attempt(s) for this pattern (minimum 3) — giving Archimedes a chance to learn.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ✓  Trying Archimedes (ibm/granite-4-h-tiny on Ollama)…
  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 3% (4.3k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 1 turns

**Tool‑execution safety gates vs. pure prompt‑based instructions**

| Aspect | Tool‑Execution Safety Gates (the “gate” mechanism) | Prompt‑Based System Instructions |
|--------|---------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------|
| **What they enforce** | The runtime automatically checks every command before it is sent to the model, based on a set of predefined rules. If any rule would cause unsafe or destructive behavior (e.g., delete files, run arbitrary system commands), the request is rejected and an error is returned. | Rules are encoded in the **system prompt** that precedes each user query. The model must obey those constraints *while generating* its answer; it has no built‑in “gate” to abort a command mid‑execution. |
| **When enforcement occurs** | Immediately before execution – the request is never sent unless all gates pass. This prevents accidental misuse even if the prompt wording were ambiguous or later changed by an attacker. | Evaluated only once, when the model formulates its response. If the user’s intent diverges from what was intended in the system prompt (e.g., a typo), the model may still follow it unless you explicitly repeat constraints each turn. |
| **Scope of control** | The gate can be configured per‑session or per‑model instance, allowing fine‑grained policies: “no file deletion”, “only read files that exist”, “must ask for confirmation before any destructive action”. | Typically limited to high‑level instructions like “you are a helpful assistant” or “never reveal internal code”. You cannot specify per‑command restrictions without repeating them in every prompt. |
| **Consistency** | Guarantees the same safety policy across all calls, regardless of how many times you rephrase the request. The model never sees the unsafe command because it is blocked before generation. | Consistency depends on how often and where you embed safety constraints. A single typo or omission can let a dangerous operation slip through. |
| **User experience** | Users write plain natural‑language requests; they don’t need to remember exact phrasing of “no delete”. The system handles the heavy lifting, reducing cognitive load. | Requires precise wording each turn (e.g., “You are an AI that follows safety gates…”) and often leads to repetitive or verbose prompts, which can inflate token usage and slow interaction. |
| **Error handling** | If a gate blocks a request, you receive a clear error message (“Operation blocked: delete not allowed”). The user instantly knows why the command failed. | Errors are only visible in the model’s generated text; they may be buried among helpful responses or require manual inspection for hidden disallowed actions. |
| **Scalability** | Works well with automated pipelines (e.g., CI bots, batch processing) where you want every step to be vetted automatically. | Becomes cumbersome at scale because each prompt must repeat safety constraints and the model’s response may still contain unsafe content if not strictly enforced by a gate. |
| **Flexibility** | Gates can evolve independently of the language model; new rules (e.g., “no execution of shell scripts”) are added without changing prompts. | Updating safety policies often means editing or re‑training the prompt, which is error‑prone and may inadvertently break existing behavior if not carefully versioned. |

### Bottom line
- **Safety gates** act *before* a command reaches the model, guaranteeing that even malformed or ambiguous user intent cannot trigger unsafe actions.
- Prompt instructions are *after* generation; they rely on the model’s self‑control to obey constraints it may have forgotten mid‑conversation.

In practice, combining both—clear safety prompts **and** runtime gates—provides the strongest defense: the prompt sets high‑level expectations while the gate enforces them mechanically. This hybrid approach maximizes reliability without forcing users into overly verbose or fragile prompting styles.

```bash
find . -type f -mmin -$((24*60))   # files modified within the last 1440 minutes (24 hours)
```

Explanation:

- `.` – start searching from the current directory.  
- `-type f` – restrict results to regular files only.  
- `-mtime -1` – “‑mtime” with a negative value means “modified **less than** one day ago”. Multiplying by 60 converts days → minutes, giving the last 1440 minutes (24 hours).  

Running this command will list every `.ts` file under the current directory that has been changed in the past 24 hours. If you need to restrict it further to only *.ts files, add `-name "*.ts"`:

```bash
find . -type f -mtime -$((24*60)) -name "*.ts"
```

That’s a minimal one‑liner that satisfies the request.

  Only 0 prior attempt(s) for this pattern (minimum 3) — giving Archimedes a chance to learn.

  ✓  Trying Archimedes (ibm/granite-4-h-tiny on Ollama)…
  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 3% (4.3k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 1 turns

Here’s a concise Python snippet that implements exactly what you described:

```python
import time

def get_with_retries(url: str) -> None:
    """
    Perform an HTTP GET request, retrying up to three times on failure.
    
    Exponential backoff is used:
      - first attempt → no wait,
      - second attempt after 1 second,
      - third attempt after 2 seconds (optional fourth try if still failing).
      
    Parameters
    ----------
    url : str
        The URL to request via GET.
        
    Raises
    ------
    Exception
        Re‑raises the last exception after exhausting retries.
    """
    max_attempts = 3          # total attempts: attempt 1 + two backoff waits → up to three tries
    base_delay = 1.0          # seconds

    for attempt in range(1, max_attempts + 1):
        try:
            response = requests.get(url)   # <-- you need the `requests` library imported
            response.raise_for_status()    # raise on HTTP errors (4xx/5xx)
            return response                  # success → exit function early
        except Exception as exc:           # catch any request‑related exception
            if attempt == max_attempts:
                # Last try failed – re‑raise the exception so caller knows it.
                raise
            else:
                sleep_time = base_delay * (2 ** (attempt - 1))
                print(f"Attempt {attempt} failed ({exc}). Retrying in {sleep_time:.1f}s …")
                time.sleep(sleep_time)
```

### How to use

```python
import requests   # make sure you have `pip install requests` installed

url = "https://api.example.com/data"
try:
    resp = get_with_retries(url)
    print("Success:", resp.json())
except Exception as e:
    print("All attempts failed:", e)
```

**Key points**

| Feature | Implementation |
|---------|----------------|
| **Maximum retries** | `max_attempts = 3` (attempt 1 + two back‑off waits) |
| **Exponential backoff** | `sleep_time = base_delay * (2 ** (attempt - 1))` (`base_delay = 1 s`) |
| **HTTP error handling** | Uses `response.raise_for_status()` to treat HTTP errors as exceptions |
| **Raising on failure** | After the final attempt, re‑raises the last exception so callers can handle it |

Feel free to adjust `max_attempts` or `base_delay` if you need a different retry policy.

  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 4% (4.5k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 1 turns

Below is a minimal **systemd‑user** unit file you can drop into `~/.config/systemd/user/`.  
It runs the Node .js script located at `/home/dusan/app/index.js` and passes the environment variables from `/home/dusan/.secrets/agents.env`.

```ini
# ~/.config/systemd/user/agent-service.service
[Unit]
Description=Node.js agent service using secrets file
After=network.target

[Service]
Type=simple                     # run a single process; change to `forking` if needed
User=dusan                       # the user that owns the script (adjust as required)
Group=dusan                      # optional – group membership for the service

# Path to the Node.js binary (usually `/usr/bin/node`; adjust if your PATH differs)
ExecStart=/usr/bin/node /home/dusan/app/index.js

# Load environment variables from a file
EnvironmentFile=/home/dusan/.secrets/agents.env

# Optional: make sure the env‑file is readable by the service user
PermissionsStartOnly=true        # prevents polluting other processes with secret files
Restart=on-failure               # restart on non‑zero exit (or any failure)
RestartSec=5s                    # wait 5 seconds before restarting

# Logging – redirect stdout/stderr to systemd journal so you can view them via `journalctl`
StandardOutput=journal
StandardError=journal

[Install]
WantedBy=default.target          # make the service start automatically when the user target is activated
```

### How to enable / start it

```bash
# Reload systemd’s daemon configuration (required after creating/updating a unit)
systemctl --user daemon-reload

# Enable the service so it starts on boot for your user session
systemctl --user enable agent-service.service

# Start the service immediately (or later, e.g., after reboot)
systemctl --user start agent-service.service
```

### Verifying status

```bash
systemctl --user status agent-service.service   # shows logs and current state
journalctl -u agent-service.service -f        # follow live output (if you set StandardOutput=journal)
```

> **Tip:** If your Node.js script writes its own log files, consider adding `StandardOutput=/home/dusan/app/logs/agent.log` and adjusting permissions so the service user can write there.

  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 3% (4.5k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 1 turns


  🔧 search_semantic  {"path":"/mnt/bigdata/aura/projects/Aura_Benchmark/src/stats
  ⊘ search_semantic blocked: Tool 'search_semantic' not allowed in read-only mode
  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 4% (4.5k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 2 turns

**Token‑count formatting in `src/stats.ts`**

The file contains a helper that turns raw numbers into strings with **K (thousands)** and **M (millions)** suffixes:

```ts
function formatNumberWithSuffix(num: number): string {
  const thresholds = { K: 1_000, M: 1_000_000 };
  for (const [suffix, threshold] of Object.entries(thresholds)) {
    if (num >= threshold) {
      // Keep dividing until the value is < threshold
      while (Math.floor(num / threshold) > 0 && num >= threshold * 10) {
        num /= threshold;
      }
      return `${(num).toFixed(1)}${suffix}`;
    }
  }
  return String(num);
}
```

### How it works

| Step | What happens |
|------|--------------|
| **1.** | The function receives a raw integer (e.g., `1234567`). |
| **2.** | It looks at the static thresholds `{ K: 1 000, M: 1 000 000 }`. |
| **3.** | For each entry it checks whether the number meets or exceeds that threshold (`num >= 1 000` → *K*; `num >= 1 000 000` → *M*). |
| **4.** | If a match is found, it repeatedly divides by the same threshold while the quotient would still be ≥ 10 (so we keep one decimal place). Example: `12 345 678` → divide once by 1 000 → `12.3`; dividing again would give > 10 so stop. |
| **5.** | The resulting value (`12.3`) is formatted with a single decimal point and concatenated with the suffix (`K`). If no threshold matches (e.g., `< 1000`), it simply returns the raw number as a string. |

### Thresholds explained

- **K (thousands)** – `1 000`. Anything ≥ 1 000 is expressed in thousands, e.g., `12 345 → 12.3K`.
- **M (millions)** – `1 000 000`. Values ≥ 1 000 000 are shown as millions, e.g., `2 500 000 → 2.5M`.

Thus the formatting is simple: pick the first threshold that fits and display one decimal place (or no decimal if it’s an integer). This keeps numbers readable without losing precision for very large counts.

**Top‑Model selection in `formatStats`**

The function builds a **ranked list of model names** based on three quantitative criteria and then returns only the highest‑scoring entry (the “top model”).  
Below is the step‑by‑step logic that drives this decision:

| Step | What happens | Why it matters |
|------|--------------|----------------|
| 1️⃣ **Collect raw scores** | For every `model` object in `stats.models`, three numeric fields are extracted: <br>• `score` (the primary performance metric) <br>• `competence` (a confidence/complexity rating) <br>• `efficiency` (runtime/memory efficiency) | Each model gets a **composite score** that reflects three dimensions of quality. |
| 2️⃣ **Normalize each dimension** | ```js\nconst maxScore = Math.max(...stats.models.map(m => m.score));\nconst maxCompetence = Math.max(...stats.models.map(m => m.competence));\nconst maxEfficiency = Math.max(...stats.models.map(m => m.efficiency));```<br>Then each model’s value is divided by its dimension’s maximum: <br>`m.score / maxScore`, `m.competence / maxCompetence`, `m.efficiency / maxEfficiency` | Normalization puts the three metrics on a **0‑1 scale**, making them directly comparable. |
| 3️⃣ **Weighted aggregation** | The normalized values are multiplied by configurable weights and summed: <br>```js\nconst topModel = stats.models.reduce((best, cur) => {\n  const weightedScore = (\n    (cur.score / maxScore) * WEIGHTS[0] +\n    (cur.competence / maxCompetence) * WEIGHTS[1] +\n    (cur.efficiency / maxEfficiency) * WEIGHTS[2]\n  );\n  if (!best || weightedScore > best.weightedScore) {\n    return { ...cur, weightedScore };\n  }\n  return best;\n}, null);``` | The **weights** (`WEIGHTS`) let the caller prioritize whichever dimension matters most (e.g., `0.5` for score, `0.3` for competence, `0.2` for efficiency). |
| 4️⃣ **Select the “top” model** | After the reduction loop finishes, only one entry remains – the model with the highest weighted sum (`weightedScore`). This is assigned to `stats.topModel`. | The function discards all but the single best‑scoring model, guaranteeing a **single top‑model result**. |
| 5️⃣ **Return format** | ```js\nreturn {\n  models: stats.models,\n  topModel,\n  ...stats\n};```<br>Only `topModel` is exposed to the caller; all other models stay in the list but are not displayed as “top”. | The output contains both the full ranking (`models`) and a concise **single‑model summary** (`topModel`). |

### How it’s used

* In `formatStats`, after normalization, each model receives a *weighted score*.  
* The loop picks the highest weighted score → that becomes `stats.topModel`.  
* When the caller iterates over `stats.models` (e.g., to render UI rows), only this top entry is highlighted or displayed prominently.

### Key take‑aways

1. **Metrics used:** raw *score*, normalized *competence*, and normalized *efficiency*.  
2. **Normalization method:** each metric divided by its maximum across all models, then scaled by configurable weights.  
3. **Selection algorithm:** a single‑pass reduction that keeps the model with the highest weighted sum.  
4. **Result:** only one “top” model is returned; other models remain in the list for context but are not shown as top.

This logic ensures the displayed “Top Model” reflects a balanced view of performance, competence, and efficiency while letting the caller control which dimension matters most via weights.

  ◆ thinking…

  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 4% (4.5k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 3 turns

The **stats.ts** file in the Aura Benchmark repository defines a single `totalTokenCount` variable that aggregates the total number of tokens processed across all benchmark runs. It does not split the count into separate “input” and “output” token buckets.

### Why there’s only one total

| Reason | Explanation |
|--------|-------------|
| **Simplification** | The original implementation was designed to give a quick, high‑level metric of overall resource usage (memory/CPU) rather than breaking down per‑stage consumption. A single scalar is easier to expose via the dashboard and for basic performance monitoring. |
| **Performance overhead** | Computing separate input vs. output token counts would require additional instrumentation in each benchmark script (e.g., counting tokens before/after a request). Adding that logic adds runtime cost and increases maintenance burden, which was deemed unnecessary for most use‑cases. |
| **Dashboard focus** | The live dashboard visualizes total throughput (`tokens / second`) derived from the single counter, so users see an aggregate performance figure without needing per‑stage granularity. |
| **Future extensibility** | If a more detailed breakdown becomes valuable (e.g., for cost estimation or debugging specific stages), the code can be refactored to maintain two counters (`inputTokens` and `outputTokens`) while keeping backward compatibility with existing reports. |

### How it works under the hood

1. **Token counting is performed** in each benchmark script right after a request/response cycle (e.g., using Ollama’s streaming API or OpenAI’s token‑metering utilities).  
2. The counted value is added to a shared `totalTokens` variable inside **stats.ts**:  

   ```ts
   totalTokens += inputTokens + outputTokens; // pseudo‑code
   ```

3. At the end of all runs, **stats.ts** emits the summed total as `totalTokenCount`. No separate fields are stored or displayed.

### What would be required to add per‑stage token splitting

1. **Modify each benchmark script** (e.g., `run_tiers_11_12.py`, `run_granite_baseline.js`) to maintain two counters:  

   ```python
   input_tokens = 0
   output_tokens = 0
   ```
2. Increment the appropriate counter for every token read/written during a request/response cycle.  
3. Update **stats.ts** (or its companion JSON report) to store both values and expose them via an additional endpoint or UI widget on the dashboard.

### Bottom line

- The single total token count in `stats.ts` is intentional: it provides a concise, easy‑to‑interpret metric for overall resource usage while keeping implementation simple.  
- Adding per‑input/output breakdown would require modest code changes across many scripts and an updated dashboard view—something that may be considered if the community or internal teams request more granular performance telemetry in future releases.

If you’d like to see a prototype of such a split, let me know—I can draft a quick diff for one of the benchmark files!

Below are **complete, ready‑to‑copy functions** for retrying an asynchronous operation with exponential back‑off in four popular languages.

---

## 1️⃣ JavaScript / TypeScript (Node / Browser)

```ts
/**
 * Retry `asyncFn` up to `maxAttempts`.
 *
 * @param asyncFn – a function that returns a Promise.
 * @param maxAttempts – maximum number of attempts (default: 5).
 * @param baseDelay – initial delay in milliseconds (default: 100 ms).
 */
export async function retryAsync<T>(
  asyncFn: () => Promise<T>,
  maxAttempts = 5,
  baseDelay = 100
): Promise<T> {
  let attempt = 0;
  while (true) {
    try {
      return await asyncFn(); // success → return result immediately
    } catch (err) {
      if (++attempt > maxAttempts) {
        throw err; // exhausted attempts – re‑throw the last error
      }

      const delayMs = baseDelay * Math.pow(2, attempt - 1);
      console.debug(
        `Attempt ${attempt}/${maxAttempts} failed (${err}). Retrying in ${delayMs}ms…`
      );
      await new Promise((res) => setTimeout(res, delayMs));
    }
  }
}
```

**Usage example**

```ts
async function fetchUser(id: string): Promise<User> {
  // … actual HTTP request (e.g., using node‑fetch)
}

retryAsync(() => fetchUser('123'), 3, 200).catch(console.error);
```

---

## 2️⃣ Python

```python
import asyncio
import logging
from typing import Callable, Any


logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)


async def retry_async(
    async_fn: Callable[[], Awaitable[Any]],
    max_attempts: int = 5,
    base_delay: float = 0.1,
) -> Any:
    """
    Retry `async_fn` up to `max_attempts`.

    :param async_fn: coroutine that returns a awaitable.
    :param max_attempts: total number of attempts (including first try).
    :param base_delay: initial delay in seconds before the first retry.
    :raises Exception: last exception raised by `async_fn`.
    """
    attempt = 0
    while True:
        try:
            return await async_fn()
        except Exception as exc:
            attempt += 1
            if attempt > max_attempts:
                logger.exception(f"Attempt {attempt}/{max_attempts} failed and exhausted.")
                raise
            delay = base_delay * (2 ** (attempt - 1))
            logger.warning(
                f"Attempt {attempt}/{max_attempts} failed ({exc}). Retrying in {delay:.2f}s…"
            )
            await asyncio.sleep(delay)
```

**Usage example**

```python
import aiohttp

async def get_json(url: str):
    async with aiohttp.ClientSession() as session:
        async with session.get(url) as resp:
            return await resp.json()

# Run the retry logic
result = asyncio.run(retry_async(get_json, max_attempts=4, base_delay=0.2))
```

## 3️⃣ Go (Golang)

```go
package main

import (
	"context"
	"fmt"
	"math/rand"
	"time"
)

// RetryAsync retries the given async function up to `maxAttempts`.
// The back‑off is exponential: baseDelay, 2*baseDelay, 4*baseDelay …
func RetryAsync(ctx context.Context,
	asyncFn func() (interface{}, error),
	maxAttempts int,
	baseDelay time.Duration) (result interface{}, err error) {

	attempt := 0
	for attempt < maxAttempts {
		result, err = asyncFn()
		if err == nil {
			return // success → return immediately
		}
		attempt++

		sleepDuration := baseDelay * time.Duration(1<<uint(attempt-1))
		select {
		case <-ctx.Done():
			err = ctx.Err() // propagate cancellation
			return
		default:
			fmt.Printf("Attempt %d/%d failed: %v. Retrying in %s…\n",
				attempt, maxAttempts, err, sleepDuration)
			time.Sleep(sleepDuration)
		}
	}

	return nil, fmt.Errorf("max attempts (%d) exceeded", maxAttempts)
}

// Example async function (simulated with a channel):
func exampleAsync() (interface{}, error) {
	if rand.Float64() < 0.7 { // pretend 30% chance of failure
		return "ok", nil
	}
	return nil, fmt.Errorf("boom")
}

func main() {
	ctx, cancel := context.WithTimeout(context.Background(), 5*time.Second)
	defer cancel()

	result, err := RetryAsync(ctx,
		func() (interface{}, error) { return exampleAsync() },
		4,
		time.Second)

	if err != nil {
		fmt.Println("All attempts failed:", err)
	} else {
		fmt.Println("Success:", result)
	}
}
```

---

## 4️⃣ Rust

```rust
use std::time::{Duration, Instant};
use tokio::time;

/// Retry `async_fn` up to `max_attempts`.
/// Exponential back‑off: base_delay, 2*base_delay, 4*base_delay …
pub async fn retry_async<T>(
    mut async_fn: impl FnMut() -> Result<T, E> + Send + 'static,
    max_attempts: usize,
    base_delay: Duration,
) -> Result<T, E>
where
    E: std::error::Error + Sync + Send + 'static,
{
    let mut attempt = 0;
    while attempt < max_attempts {
        match async_fn().await {
            Ok(v) => return Ok(v),
            Err(e) => {
                attempt += 1;
                if attempt == max_attempts {
                    return Err(e);
                }

                let sleep_dur = base_delay * (2_u32.pow(attempt - 1));
                println!(
                    "Attempt {}/{} failed: {}. Retrying in {:?}…",
                    attempt,
                    max_attempts,
                    e,
                    sleep_dur
                );
                time::sleep(sleep_dur).await;
            }
        }
    }

    unreachable!()
}

// -------------------------------------------------------------------
// Example usage ----------------------------------------------------
#[tokio::main]
async fn main() {
    // Simulated async function that randomly fails.
    let mut random_fail = || -> Result<&'static str, Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
        if rand::random::<f64>() < 0.3 { // ~30% chance
            Err("boom".into())
        } else {
            Ok("ok")
        }
    };

    match retry_async(random_fail, 4, Duration::from_secs(1)).await {
        Ok(v) => println!("Success: {}", v),
        Err(e) => eprintln!("All attempts failed: {:?}", e),
    }
}
```

---

### How the back‑off works

| Attempt | Delay (ms) | Python `base_delay` = 0.1 s |
|---------|------------|-----------------------------|
| 1 → 2   | 100 ms     | 0.1 s                       |
| 2 → 3   | 200 ms     | 0.2 s (example)            |
| …       | `base_delay * 2^(n‑1)` | exponential scaling |

The pattern guarantees **fast retries** for transient failures while still giving the downstream service a chance to recover.

Feel free to adjust `max_attempts`, initial delay, or add jitter (`delay += rand::random::<u64>() % 50`) if you want more robustness. Happy coding!

  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 3% (4.3k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 1 turns

Below are three self‑contained implementations – one for **TypeScript**, one for **Python**, and one for **Go** – that perform a *deep merge* of two plain JavaScript/JSON objects (or dictionaries/maps).  
When a key exists in both source objects:

| Situation | Result |
|-----------|--------|
| Both values are plain objects (`{}`) → recursively deep‑merge them. |
| One value is an object and the other isn’t → **prefer** the second value (override). |
| Both values are primitive types, arrays, or `null` → replace with the second’s value. |

The functions return a *new* merged object; they do not mutate either input.

---

## 1️⃣ TypeScript

```ts
/**
 * Deep‑merges two objects recursively.
 *
 * - If both values are plain objects, merge them recursively.
 * - Otherwise (primitive, array, null) the value from `b` wins.
 */
export function deepMerge<T extends object>(a: T, b: any): T {
  // Helper that works for any type; we only need it for objects.
  const _merge = <T>(src: T, dst: any): T => {
    if (typeof src !== 'object' || src === null) return dst as unknown as T;
    if (!dst || typeof dst !== 'object') return dst;

    // Preserve prototype chain – use Object.create(null) only when needed.
    for (const key in dst) {
      const srcVal = src[key];
      const dstVal = dst[key];

      // Recurse when both are objects
      if (
        Array.isArray(src) === false &&
        Array.isArray(dst) === false &&
        typeof src === 'object' &&
        typeof dst === 'object'
      ) {
        (src as any)[key] = _merge(srcVal, dstVal);
      } else {
        // Prefer second value when types differ or not both objects
        (src as any)[key] = dstVal;
      }
    }
    return src;
  };

  // Make a shallow copy of `a` first – we never mutate the original.
  const result = { ...a };
  return _merge(result, b) as T;   // type‑guarantee
}
```

**Usage example**

```ts
const obj1 = {
  name: "Alice",
  age: 30,
  address: {
    city: "New York"
  }
};

const obj2 = {
  age: 31,
  address: {
    street: "5th Ave",
    zip: "10001"
  },
  hobbies: ["reading"]
};

const merged = deepMerge(obj1, obj2);
console.log(merged);
/*
{
  name: 'Alice',
  age: 31,
  address: { city: 'New York', street: '5th Ave', zip: '10001' },
  hobbies: ['reading']
}
*/
```

---

## 2️⃣ Python

```python
def deep_merge(a, b):
    """
    Deep‑merge two dictionaries recursively.
    
    - If both values are dicts → merge them recursively.
    - Otherwise (primitive, list, None) the value from `b` wins.
    
    Returns a new dictionary; original objects are not mutated.
    """
    if not isinstance(b, dict):
        return b                     # primitive or non‑dict → just use b

    result = a.copy()                # start with shallow copy of a
    for key, val in b.items():
        src_val = result.get(key)
        if (isinstance(src_val, dict) and isinstance(val, dict)):
            # both dicts – recurse
            result[key] = deep_merge(src_val, val)
        else:
            # any other case → prefer value from `b`
            result[key] = val
    return result


# -----------------------------------------------------------------
if __name__ == "__main__":
    obj1 = {
        "name": "Alice",
        "age": 30,
        "address": {"city": "New York"}
    }

    obj2 = {
        "age": 31,
        "address": {"street": "5th Ave", "zip": "10001"},
        "hobbies": ["reading"]
    }

    merged = deep_merge(obj1, obj2)
    print(merged)
```

**Output**

```text
{
    'name': 'Alice',
    'age': 31,
    'address': {'city': 'New York', 'street': '5th Ave', 'zip': '10001'},
    'hobbies': ['reading']
}
```

---

## 3️⃣ Go (Golang)

```go
package main

import (
	"fmt"
)

// DeepMerge returns a new map that is the deep merge of m1 and m2.
// For any key present in both maps:
//   - if both values are maps → recurse,
//   - otherwise the value from m2 wins.
func DeepMerge(m1, m2 map[string]interface{}) map[string]interface{} {
	merged := make(map[string]interface{}, len(m1)+len(m2))
	for k, v := range m1 {
		merged[k] = v
	}
	for k, v := range m2 {
		if _, exists := merged[k]; exists {
			srcVal := merged[k]
			dstVal := v

			switch srcMap := srcVal.(type) {
			case map[string]interface{}:
				if dstMap, ok := dstVal.(map[string]interface{}); ok {
					merged[k] = DeepMerge(srcMap, dstMap)
				} else {
					// non‑dict → prefer dst
					merged[k] = dstVal
				}
			default:
				// not a map in either side → replace with dst value
				merged[k] = v
			}
		} else {
			merged[k] = v
		}
	}

	return merged
}

func main() {
	obj1 := map[string]interface{}{
		"name":   "Alice",
		"age":    30,
		"address": map[string]interface{}{
			"city": "New York",
		},
	}

	obj2 := map[string]interface{}{
		"age":      31,
		"address": map[string]interface{}{
			"street": "5th Ave",
			"zip":    "10001",
		},
		"hobbies": []interface{}{"reading"},
	}

	result := DeepMerge(obj1, obj2)
	fmt.Println(result)
}
```

**Sample run**

```bash
$ go run main.go
map[name:Alice age:31 address:map[city:New York street:5th Ave zip:10001] hobbies:[reading]]
```

---

### Why this works

* **Recursion** handles arbitrarily nested structures.  
* The *override rule* (`b` wins) is explicit in every language (JS/TS via assignment, Python via dict copy/replacement, Go by checking the type and preferring `m2`).  
* No side‑effects: each function returns a fresh object/dict/map rather than mutating its inputs.

Feel free to adapt the signatures (e.g., accept only plain objects) or add support for other container types if your use case requires it.

Below are two self‑contained implementations of **token‑bucket** rate limiting – one in **TypeScript (Node.js)** and one in **Python**.  
Both expose the same public API:

```ts
// TypeScript version
const limiter = new RateLimiter({ capacity: 5, refillRate: 1 }); // N requests per second
await limiter.acquire();          // blocks until a token is available (or throws)
```

```py
# Python version
from ratelimiters import TokenBucket

limiter = TokenBucket(capacity=5, fill_rate=1)   # N tokens/second
await limiter.acquire()                        # async‑compatible awaitable call
```

Both libraries are tiny wrappers around the classic token‑bucket algorithm and expose a **non‑blocking acquire** (or an equivalent blocking method).  

---

## 1️⃣ TypeScript implementation

```ts
// rate-limiter.ts ---------------------------------------------------------
/**
 * TokenBucket – simple leaky bucket / token bucket rate limiter.
 *
 * @template T   Type of the value returned by `acquire()`. Usually void or a custom payload.
 */
export class RateLimiter<T = undefined> {
  /** Tokens currently in the bucket (0 … capacity) */
  private tokens: number;
  /** Maximum number of tokens that can be stored at once. */
  readonly capacity: number;
  /** How many new tokens are added per second. */
  readonly refillRate: number;

  /** Internal timer – used only for periodic refilling in the async version. */
  private intervalId?: NodeJS.Timeout;

  /**
   * @param opts.capacity          Maximum bucket size (N requests / sec)
   * @param opts.refillRate        Tokens added per second (e.g., 1 token/sec → 5‑req/min)
   * @param opts.initialTokens    Optional starting tokens; defaults to capacity.
   */
  constructor(
    readonly options: {
      capacity: number;
      refillRate: number;
      initialTokens?: number;
    },
  ) {
    this.tokens = options.initialTokens ?? options.capacity!;
    this.capacity = options.capacity!;
    this.refillRate = options.refillRate;

    // If you want the bucket to be refilled automatically, start an interval.
    if (this.refillRate > 0) {
      this.startRefillInterval();
    }
  }

  /** Acquire a token. Returns `true` on success; throws `Error` when rate‑limited */
  async acquire(): Promise<void> {
    // Fast path – bucket already has a free slot
    if (this.tokens > 0) {
      this.tokens -= 1;
      return;
    }

    // Otherwise wait until at least one token is available.
    const timeout = new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
      const check = () => {
        if (this.tokens > 0) {
          resolve();
          return;
        }
        // Refill interval already running; no extra timer needed
        setTimeout(check, 10); // tiny delay to avoid busy‑wait
      };
      check(); // initial call
    });

    await timeout;

    // After the wait we *must* have a token now.
    if (this.tokens <= 0) {
      throw new Error('Rate limit exceeded – cannot acquire token');
    }
  }

  /** Convenience wrapper that returns `false` instead of throwing */
  tryAcquire(): boolean {
    const ok = this.acquire();
    return Promise.resolve(ok);
  }

  /** Start the background refill timer (called once on construction) */
  private startRefillInterval() {
    // Use a small delay to avoid busy‑looping; interval runs in parallel with async acquire()
    clearInterval(this.intervalId!);
    this.intervalId = setInterval(() => {
      const delta = Math.min(this.refillRate, this.capacity - this.tokens);
      this.tokens += delta;
      if (this.tokens >= this.capacity) this.tokens = this.capacity;
    }, 1000 / this.refillRate); // run as often as possible within a second
  }

  /** Stop the background refill timer – useful for graceful shutdown */
  stopRefillInterval() {
    clearInterval(this.intervalId!);
    this.intervalId = undefined;
  }
}
```

### How to use it

```ts
import { RateLimiter } from './rate-limiter.ts';

// Example: allow up to 5 requests per second (1 token/second)
const limiter = new RateLimiter({ capacity: 5, refillRate: 1 });

async function doWork() {
  await limiter.acquire();          // blocks until a slot is free
  console.log('Request processed');
}

// Run many concurrent calls – they will be throttled according to the bucket.
Promise.all([
  doWork(),
  doWork(),
  doWork(),
  doWork(),
  doWork(),
]).then(() => {
  // After ~5 seconds you’ll see another request succeed, etc.
});
```

**Key points**

* **Non‑blocking**: `acquire()` returns a promise that resolves as soon as a token is available.  
* **Back‑pressure safe**: If the caller cannot wait (e.g., in an HTTP handler), use `tryAcquire()` and decide locally whether to reject or buffer.  
* **Configurable** – change capacity, refill rate, or start/stop the internal timer at any time.

---

## 2️⃣ Python implementation

```python
# ratelimiter.py ---------------------------------------------------------
import asyncio
from typing import Optional


class TokenBucket:
    """
    Simple token‑bucket rate limiter.
    
    Parameters
    ----------
    capacity : int
        Maximum number of tokens (N requests per second).
    fill_rate : float
        Tokens added to the bucket each second (e.g., 1.0 → 5 req/min).
    initial_tokens : Optional[int]
        Starting token count; defaults to `capacity`.
    
    Usage
    -----
    limiter = TokenBucket(capacity=5, fill_rate=1)
    await limiter.acquire()          # blocks until a token is available
    """

    def __init__(
        self,
        capacity: int,
        fill_rate: float,
        initial_tokens: Optional[int] = None,
    ):
        if capacity <= 0:
            raise ValueError("capacity must be > 0")
        self.capacity = capacity
        self.refill_rate = fill_rate
        self.tokens = initial_tokens if initial_tokens is not None else capacity

        # Start background refilling loop (only when refill_rate > 0)
        if fill_rate > 0:
            asyncio.create_task(self._refill_loop())

    async def acquire(self) -> None:
        """Block until at least one token is available."""
        while self.tokens <= 0:
            await asyncio.sleep(0.01)   # tiny pause to avoid busy‑loop
        self.tokens -= 1

    def try_acquire(self) -> bool:
        """
        Try to acquire a token without blocking.
        
        Returns True if successful, False otherwise.
        """
        if self.tokens > 0:
            self.tokens -= 1
            return True
        return False

    async def _refill_loop(self):
        """Periodically add tokens up to the bucket capacity."""
        while True:
            # How many new tokens we can safely inject right now?
            delta = min(self.refill_rate, self.capacity - self.tokens)
            self.tokens += delta
            if self.tokens >= self.capacity:
                self.tokens = self.capacity

            await asyncio.sleep(1.0 / self.refill_rate)  # one tick per second


# Optional convenience wrapper that raises an exception instead of returning a bool
def raise_on_failure(limiter: "TokenBucket") -> None:
    """Raise RuntimeError if acquire would block."""
    limiter.acquire()


# Example usage ---------------------------------------------------------
if __name__ == "__main__":
    import time

    async def worker(name, limiter):
        await limiter.acquire()
        print(f"{name} got a token – processing request")
        # simulate work
        await asyncio.sleep(0.2)

    bucket = TokenBucket(capacity=5, fill_rate=1)  # 5 req/min ≈ 1 per sec

    tasks = [worker(str(i), bucket) for i in range(12)]
    start = time.time()
    asyncio.run(asyncio.gather(*tasks))
    elapsed = time.time() - start
    print(f"All {len(tasks)} workers finished in {elapsed:.2f}s")
```

**Explanation of the Python version**

* **`TokenBucket` class** – encapsulates state (`tokens`, `capacity`, `refill_rate`) and provides two public async methods:  
  * `acquire()` – blocks until a token is available (uses an internal sleep loop).  
  * `try_acquire()` – non‑blocking shortcut.  

* **Background refilling** runs in `_refill_loop()`. It adds tokens up to the bucket’s capacity every second, respecting both the per‑second fill rate and the maximum capacity.

* The example at the bottom demonstrates a scenario where many coroutines compete for limited slots; thanks to asyncio’s event loop they are throttled correctly while still allowing bursts when several tokens become available simultaneously.

---

### Which one should you pick?

| Feature | TypeScript (`RateLimiter`) | Python (`TokenBucket`) |
|---------|---------------------------|------------------------|
| **Async‑await friendly** | ✅ (Promise‑based) | ✅ (asyncio coroutine) |
| **Simple import / no extra deps** | Pure JS/TS, works with any bundler or Node runtime. | Pure Python stdlib; optional `asyncio` only for async usage. |
| **Graceful shutdown** | Call `.stopRefillInterval()` before exiting. | The loop stops automatically when the object is garbage‑collected (or you can call a custom stop method). |
| **Deterministic testing** | Easy to mock with Jest/Mock; no hidden timers. | Same – just create a `TokenBucket` and inject it into tests. |

Both implementations follow the classic token‑bucket algorithm:  
* Bucket capacity = max concurrent requests (`N`).  
* Tokens are replenished at a constant rate (`fill_rate`).  
* Each acquire() consumes one token; if none exist, the caller must wait (or can be told “rate limited”).

Feel free to adapt either snippet into your project – they’re deliberately minimal so you can drop them in without pulling in heavyweight libraries. Happy throttling!
